Session 2 of the review-remnants plan closed findings #50 and #57 -- the two of five "deliberately NOT applied" findings from the original v3-pre-release review that turned out to be genuinely fixable this pass, once the earlier "needs an architectural addition" framing was tested against the real code rather than taken at face value.

#50 (`utils/cpPackages.js`'s return-value spread) turned out not to need an architecture change at all -- just a careful test-first repoint of every flattened-field read site before removing the spread, so a weakened-not-removed read site would fail loudly instead of silently passing. #57 (`utils/colorPalette.js`'s cross-view double-fetch) was the harder one: the function's own comments call its internal ordering load-bearing, so the fix wasn't trusted on a code read. A new instrumented fixture (`scripts/colorPaletteCrossViewFetch.test.js`) stubbed the two network-touching dependencies via `require.cache`, was deliberately run against the *pre-fix* code first to confirm it actually caught the redundancy (4 calls logged), then re-run post-fix to confirm exactly 2 -- proof, not a plausible-looking diff.

A completeness-sweep prompt after the first "done" report caught something neither the code review nor `npm test` could see: `docs/db-deferred-list.md`'s tracking bullet still listed #48 and #49 as "deliberately NOT applied," even though both had shipped in the *previous* release (v3.46.0/PR #154) on a separate branch that never came back to update this line. The session's own first-draft summary count inherited that stale baseline and was wrong as a result -- caught only because the sweep asked to re-derive the count from each item's actual state rather than trust the file's existing total.

The same sweep, run again before merge at Harkirat's explicit request, surfaced a real CI-only bug: `scripts/reflow-comments.mjs`'s syntax-check step (`execFileSync("node", ["--check", "/dev/stdin"], ...)`) crashed on this repo's GitHub Actions Linux runner with `ENOENT ... /proc/<pid>/fd/socket:[...]` -- a `/dev/stdin`-as-socket Node quirk that doesn't reproduce on macOS. It only fires for a file with a genuinely proposed change, which is exactly why three separate local `npm test` runs never caught it: the triggering file was untracked (`git ls-files` skips untracked paths) during every one of them, so the checker never actually saw it until it was staged and CI ran against tracked content for the first time. Filed as its own bug rather than fixed here, to keep this PR scoped to the review findings -- but it's a real trap for any future PR that adds or edits a `.js`/`.mjs`/`.sh` file: passing `npm test` locally proves nothing about this specific check if the changed file was still untracked when you ran it.

## 2026-08-18 15:34 EDT — A shutdown race that finding #12 never actually closed (v3.49.0-pre)

Session 3 of the review-remnants plan, and the last one: finding #56, kept isolated in its own session from the start because it directly intersects finding #12's shutdown-race fix, and the plan's own reasoning said folding it in beside unrelated work would recreate the exact stacking risk the original review flagged.

The ask was mechanical on paper: `utils/eventStore.js`'s `SearchTerm` upsert was the one write in the event plane still issuing a standalone `updateOne` per closed search session instead of going through the `FLUSH_AT` buffer everything else uses. Batch it into a `bulkWrite`, hook it into the same shutdown call site finding #12 already fixed, done.

Re-reading finding #12's fix fresh — the handoff's own instruction, not a nice-to-have — is what turned this from mechanical into interesting. `installShutdownFlush()` (eventStore.js) and `releaseLock` (instanceLock.js) both register listeners on the same `SIGTERM`/`SIGINT`. Node calls every listener for one signal synchronously, back-to-back, in registration order. `flushSearchSessions()`/`flushEvents()` splice their buffers the instant they're called — before any `await`. So whichever listener ran SECOND always found the buffers already drained by the first, and its own flush resolved via pure microtasks — which Node drains before the FIRST listener's real, macrotask-bound Mongo write can complete. The second listener's `process.exit()` was firing before the real write landed, no matter how the buffering was designed. Finding #12's fix only closed this for the narrow pre-`ClientReady` window where `releaseLock` is the only listener registered; the realistic post-boot case, where both listeners coexist, was never actually fixed.

This is the kind of bug that hides behind a green test suite forever — `npm test` cannot exercise a real process receiving a real signal, so nothing would ever have caught it short of a live SIGTERM against a real Mongo connection. The fix itself is small once found: memoize the shutdown flush into one shared promise, `flushAllForShutdown()`, that every listener awaits instead of each independently re-invoking flush functions that are individually idempotent-but-fast-no-op on a second call.

What made this a "prove it, don't reason about it" situation rather than a "this looks right" one was a disposable harness against real local dev Mongo — not the stubbed model `scripts/eventStore.test.js` uses. It registered the exact pre-memoization shape (two independent listeners, real flush functions, unmemoized) and sent itself a real `SIGTERM`: the write was silently dropped, confirmed absent via `mongosh`. Same harness, memoized code path: the write landed, four times in a row. That pairing — a reproduction that can fail, and a fix that reliably doesn't — is what actually closes a shutdown race, as opposed to a design that merely looks closed.

One more thing worth naming: mid-session, a `docs-audit` run flagged two broken cross-references that turned out to belong to a completely different, currently-running peer session's uncommitted work sitting in the same shared working tree — not mine, and not something to touch or discard. Isolating it with a scoped `git stash`/`pop` confirmed this branch's own commit was clean, and it surfaced a real version-number collision (that other session had already claimed `v3.49.0-pre` for its own feature) that got resolved by asking rather than guessing.

## 2026-08-18 20:44 EDT — The reflow-comments CI crash, and a /help wording pick that turned out to be a decision already made (v3.50.0-pre)

Two loose ends from the review-remnants plan, picked up in the same session right after #56 merged, neither substantial enough for its own numbered session.

The first was a real bug with a known fix sitting filed and untouched: `scripts/reflow-comments.mjs`'s syntax-check step crashed on GitHub Actions' Linux runners, for any file with a genuinely proposed change, ever since it started being exercised on tracked content. Session 2 found it the hard way — its own new test file tripped the crash on CI after passing locally three times — and deliberately filed it rather than fixing it inline, to keep that PR from scope-creeping into the reflow tooling. The mechanism was already understood: `node --check /dev/stdin` (and `bash -n /dev/stdin`) breaks on that runner with an `ENOENT` on a `/proc/<pid>/fd/socket` path, because Node's `/dev/stdin` resolution assumes a regular pipe and that CI environment's process wiring apparently hands it a socket instead. The fix is the standard workaround for this class of bug: write the content to a real temp file and check that path instead. What made it worth doing carefully rather than just patching the two `execFileSync` calls was that the function had zero direct test coverage before this — the crash was invisible locally precisely because nothing exercised the code path that only CI's Linux runner disagreed with. Six new cases close that gap, including one that doesn't just check syntax-checking still works, but greps the module's own source for any *code* line (as opposed to a comment describing the old bug) still referencing `/dev/stdin` — a regression here should fail structurally, not probabilistically.

The second was a UX question that looked like it needed a fresh decision and turned out not to. `/help`'s three inconsistent-looking treatments for "a command with no options beyond visibility" got asked about directly — and the instinct to just pick one style and apply it everywhere would have quietly undone something Harkirat had already decided on 2026-08-10: repeating a per-command Options header on the admin pages read as overwhelming, so he collapsed it to one shared footnote and cut that section roughly in half. That reasoning is recorded in the code's own comments, not just in memory — which is exactly why it was worth reading before answering instead of just going with the recommendation. Filed back with the actual shape of the remaining question (one line's wording, not a structural inconsistency) folded into a `/help` design pass Harkirat already has planned, rather than resolved as a standalone pick that would have reverted a deliberate readability call.
## 2026-08-18 14:01 EDT — The page was misaligned because its CSS was never shared, and the phone bug was a declaration order (v3.51.0)

Harkirat's report was four words long — *"the entire page is misaligned all over the page (look at nav and footer)"* — and the interesting part is that every automated gate on the site was green while it was true.

The `/commands` page had shipped a `<header class="bar">` with the wordmark, the tab switcher and the three buttons as direct children. The obvious read is that it needed the `<nav>` wrapper `shell()` uses, because `.bar nav{margin-left:auto}` is what pushes the controls to the right edge. That was true, and it was not the cause. Probing the built file for the rules themselves found **none of them**: `public/commands.html` carried zero `.bar`, zero `.bar nav`, zero `.page`, zero `#prog`. The header was an unstyled block element.

The reason is that `.bar` and `.page` were never shared. They were written out verbatim inside `shell()`, again inside `warmShell()`, and a third time inside `chronicle.js` — byte-identical in all three, differing only in `#prog`'s accent variable. Every family that had ever needed the chrome had simply carried its own copy, so nothing marked the moment a fourth family arrived with the markup and without the styling. The build has thirteen self-checks. Not one of them asks whether a class in the markup resolves to a rule in the stylesheet, and that is a genuinely hard thing to check in general — which is why the fix was to remove the opportunity rather than to add a fourteenth gate. `CHROME.BAR_CSS` and `CHROME.PAGE_CSS` now exist, all three copies point at them, and the other eight pages came out byte-identical apart from one CSS comment that became a JS comment.

The second bug was worse and was only found by measuring the real thing. At a 390px touch viewport the picker reported `data-open="0"`, `list` at zero height, and the button that reopens it at `display:none`. `setOpen()` collapses the picker below 880px on purpose — sixteen rows is a lot of phone. But `.cx-fold{display:none}` sat *after* the `@media (max-width:880px)` block that shows it, and both selectors are `(0,1,0)`, so source order decided and the base rule won at every width. The button had never been visible, anywhere, since the page was written. A phone got a search field, no list, and no way to get one — on the page whose entire reason to exist is that someone needs help.

Nothing catches a declaration-order bug by reading. It looks correct in the source, the media query is right there above it, and the gates pass because the markup, the links and the contrast are all fine. What caught it was opening the page at the width a reader would actually use and asking the DOM what it computed — and even that took two attempts, because the in-app browser pane had gone `document.hidden`, so screenshots painted blank while `elementFromPoint` still answered correctly. Falling back to headless Chrome introduced its own trap: `--window-size=390` clamps the *layout* viewport to a minimum of 500px, so the "mobile" screenshot was a 500px layout cropped to 390 and looked like text was being clipped. A three-line probe page reporting `innerWidth` settled it in one shot. Two different rendering surfaces, two different ways of returning well-formed numbers about the wrong thing.

The design pass that followed is the part Harkirat actually asked for, and it deliberately spent almost nothing on colour. The changelog pages already paid for that lesson — three accent colours read as one template in three shades, and what finally separated them was the grid. So: the Composer moved out of the reading column to span the page and dock flush under the bar, becoming the one loud element and the thing the page is remembered by; bays split into a prose zone and a data zone so a reader knows where to stop reading and start scanning; a continuous hairline down the option block puts every option name at the same x; guides took the display face and commands kept mono, so a concept and an invocation stop looking like the same object. And `visibility` — which all fourteen commands carry — stopped printing "Who sees the answer" fourteen times, which had been quietly burying the options that actually differ between one command and the next.

## 2026-08-18 23:12 EDT — The /commands page, redesigned three times, and the fix was a palette the bot already shipped (v3.51.0)

Harkirat looked at the `/commands` page and said it was "boring, bland, and kind of unintuitive/confusing" — and then rejected two more rounds after that. Three rejections in one evening is worth writing down properly, because each one failed differently and only the third failure was about design taste.

**Round one was invalidated rather than judged.** Three spatial directions — a two-column ledger, a cross-reference index, a sticky group counter — all built from the real catalog with identical chrome so that only the layout differed. Every one of them contained the **Composer**: a page-wide monospace command line with a blinking caret that was read-only. Harkirat had been trying to type into it *every single time he opened the site*. So the round varied the layout while the actual defect sat inside all three options, and the comparison could not have produced a useful answer whichever one he picked. **A controlled comparison is only controlled for the variables you actually varied** — a defect held constant across every arm is invisible to the experiment, and it is exactly the thing an experiment is least likely to surface.

**Round two was rejected on sight, and my own diagnosis was harsher than his.** With the Composer gone I built three more: a plain-language ask index, a "rack" that drew each command's options as slots, and both together. All three were **a single column you scroll**, differing only in how much list sat above the fold — hairline rules, tiny uppercase mono labels, a dense one-column field. That is verbatim one of the three looks AI-generated design currently defaults to, and I arrived at it while congratulating myself on avoiding the other two. **A tidier list is not a spatial idea.** The page had been diagnosed as having no spatial idea and I answered with better typography.

**The thing that fixed it was free and had been sitting there for three rounds.** The bot already ships a per-command accent — Calendar slate `#3A5068`, Draws plum `#6B4E7D`, Draw Prices emerald `#1F8A5E`, Patch Notes gold `#F2C230`, Season End amber `#F2994A`, Timestamp teal `#17A2A2`, `/help` and `/invite` coral. Every attempt so far had painted fourteen commands in one Signal Green while the product itself used eight colours. Using the real ones is not decoration, it is **recognition**: a reader who has seen `/patch notes` come back gold in Discord knows the panel before reading its name. And six of the fourteen have no fixed colour *by design* — they derive one per render from what you asked for — so the page draws that as a hollow dot rather than inventing six hexes to make the set look complete.

### The design that landed

**The Bench.** Two zones, both always on screen. You do not scroll a list of commands; you pick one from a dot column on the left and a panel fills on the right, wearing that command's own colour as a rule across the top and a soft aura behind it. Its resting state is the ask index, so one object answers both *I know exactly which command I want* and *I have no idea what this thing does*. Options are drawn as slots — a Call of Duty gunsmith build is a weapon with named slots you fill, and a slash command is a command with named options you fill, which is the same shape and one every reader of this page already knows cold. The page never says "attachment"; the metaphor is the reason for the form, not a label printed on it.

**The whole interaction is `:target`, so the page needs no JavaScript.** Every command has a real URL, deep links work, and the core function survives with scripting off — which the previous filter-driven page did not. The home panel sits last in the DOM so a following-sibling selector can hide it, rather than reaching for `:has()` and making the rendering depend on a selector feature behaving identically across engines.

### Lesson

**A colour chosen for one medium is not a colour, it is a starting point — and the ground it has to survive is not the one you assume.** The eight command accents were picked for a Discord embed, so each is solved to a text-safe variant per theme by walking its lightness until it *measures* 4.5:1, rather than being run through the site's existing 38%-mix formula, which this repo already records as turning saturated hues to mud. The first version solved both themes against `--desk`, the page surface. Three colours then measured **4.00, 4.05 and 4.08 on `--raised`** — the surface the panel actually paints — while reporting 4.60, 4.66 and 4.69 against the desk and passing. Dark theme's card is *lighter* than its page, so for near-white text the card is the harder ground; light theme's page is *darker* than its card, so there it is the page. **Tune against the harder background of each theme, and know which one that is rather than assuming it is the same surface in both.** Four test cases now assert all thirty-two pairs, including one that feeds the solver a colour it cannot possibly fix — because a solver that silently returns black unchanged would make every other assertion pass on something nobody can read.

## 2026-08-19 14:03 EDT — Four root causes, and a colour strategy that could not fail (v3.51.0)

Harkirat annotated ten screenshots of the `/commands` page and the twenty-nine notes on them reduced to four root causes. Three of the four were things I had built deliberately and got wrong on the merits — which is a different and more useful kind of failure than an oversight, because each one had a reason behind it that survived review and was still wrong.

**The colour one is the one worth keeping.** The page paints each command in the bot's own accent, and those hexes are chosen for a Discord embed rather than a web page, so each was solved to a text-safe variant by walking its lightness until it measured 4.5:1. Patch Gold `#F2C230` came out `#7C5F08` — a thirty-one point lightness drop, which is brown. Neon Amber came out rust. And forty pixels away, in the index dot, sat the raw hex, because a dot is a fill and needs no solving. So the page was showing two different colours for one command, side by side, on the strength of an argument about *recognition*. Every gate passed, because every gate was asking about contrast and contrast was fine.

The mistake was not the solver. It was **using an identity colour as a text colour at all**. A brand hue and an accessible body-text colour are two different jobs, and no single value holds both — the moment you demand 4.5:1 from a hue, you have agreed to give up whatever amount of the hue that costs, and for a saturated mid-tone on a light ground that is most of it. Turn it around and use the accent as a *fill*, then solve the *foreground* against it, and the problem inverts into one that cannot fail: solve the crossover where black and white are equally bad and it sits at relative luminance 0.1791, where the better of the two still measures **4.58:1**. There is no fill in existence that a solved foreground cannot sit on. The old approach had no floor at all; this one has a proof.

That floor turns out to be sensitive to exactly one decision. Using the site's near-black `#16131B` instead of pure black drops it from 4.58 to 4.27, and CP Emerald `#1F8A5E` lands in the gap at 4.33 against white and 4.25 against near-black — the single hex in the palette that fails. So the test that guards this feeds it the worst grey there is, and it is the case that breaks the moment somebody softens that ink for looking too harsh.

**Then there was the half of the page nobody had measured.** All ten screenshots were desktop. Asked how far the redesign should go, Harkirat picked a fifth architecture and gave the reason: *"because i forgot to mention how poorly it behaves and looks on mobile."* Measured at 375×812, the design that had been chosen from renders the day before put the command index — the page's entire navigation — **855px tall, beginning 2.3 screenfuls down**, below a home panel of 1505px. One command's panel was 1282px and its command line, the artifact the whole page exists to hand over, sat at y=1339. The design's premise was *two zones, both always on screen*. On a phone there were no zones; there was a stack, and the stack was the single scrolling column that had been rejected two rounds earlier.

**A design whose central claim is false in the mode most readers use is not one that needs tuning.** The rebuild has one shape in both modes — a rail that wraps on a wide screen and scrolls on a narrow one, and a command line that is sticky in both — and the numbers moved accordingly: document height 3010px to 1219px, panel 1282px to 660px, index 855px to a 58px rail.

**And one bug that only a person could have found.** Opening `/timestamp` showed `datetime sun 4:30pm` in the copy line; picking a timezone replaced it with `timezone (UTC−04:00) Eastern`. The required option was gone. The repaint built the line from the set of values the reader had picked, and a free-text required option can never be in that set, so the page silently handed out a command Discord rejects. Harkirat caught it across two screenshots. No test covered a repaint, and no gate could have: every check on that page verifies what the *build* emits, and the defect only existed after a click.

### Lesson

**A gate proves the property it measures, and nothing about whether that is the property that matters.** The contrast gate asserted 4.5:1 on thirty-two pairs and passed on brown. The build gates asserted the emitted line was correct and it was — the line went wrong on the first interaction. Both were real checks that ran, and both were answering a question one level away from the one a reader has. The fix in each case was to move the assertion to where the reader stands: solve the foreground against the fill *because that is what is painted*, and build the line from the option list *because a reader wants a command that runs*, then test the worst case rather than the current data. A test whose expected answer is "everything currently in the repo" cannot tell you the approach is sound; it can only tell you today's inputs happen to clear it.

## 2026-08-19 17:45 EDT — True but useless, and a generator that cheerfully wrote the wrong file (v3.51.0)

A third round of critique on the same page, and the useful finding was not in the screenshots — it was that several of the defects they caught were ones I had introduced while fixing the round before.

Round two ended with me adding Discord's own registered option description to every option, on the reasoning that it is authoritative: it is the exact sentence the client shows a reader a second after they leave the page, so it cannot be wrong. That reasoning was sound and the result was that an option printed its description **three times** — Discord's, then the hand-written one I had not removed, then that same one again in the free-text branch. Three sentences that agreed, stacked under one option name. I had added a layer without deleting the layer it superseded, and then verified the new layer rendered rather than verifying what the option now looked like.

The same shape produced the "IN DISCORD" line, which restates the purpose line directly above it, and it produced the sentence `No options — just run it.` on `/invite`, which is false: `visibility` is an option, the list filtered it out, and the empty-check ran after the filter, so the page asserted a command had no options while the option it had just hidden sat three lines below in a footnote.

**The pattern is one thing.** I had been shipping elements because they were ACCURATE and asking to have them judged; the test being applied was whether they TEACH or DO anything. Accuracy is the floor, not a reason to include. Three rounds line up behind that: the hollow dot for the six colour-varying commands was true and read as *missing*; the conic sweep that replaced it was true and read as a rainbow bauble; `IN DISCORD` was true and said nothing new. A page is not a set of claims to be checked, it is a set of things a reader either gains from or pays for.

**And the thing that had been asked for all along was a simulator, not a reference.** Across three rounds the only element ever praised was the card changing colour when a weapon category is tapped — the one moment the page behaves like the bot. Every unmet request pointed the same way: why does the weapon field not autocomplete like the bot, why can I not click the option blocks in the command bar the way I would in Discord, why is there no clear, why can I not copy the bare command. I had been building a reference you can poke.

So the page now searches the same weapon list the bot searches. The site builds with no database, which is exactly why it could not before — so an exporter dumps 68 MP and 7 DMZ weapons into a committed JSON that the page inlines. Typing "hol" finds Holger 26, and an exact match re-tints the whole panel to that weapon's real category colour. That artifact is the first thing on this page that can go stale, and everything else on it is read from the bot's own builders at build time precisely so it cannot; a test now fails on a missing, malformed or implausibly small file.

**Which the exporter needed immediately.** Its first run grouped on `weapon` where the schema says `weaponName`, collapsed every document into one null-keyed bucket, printed "wrote 1 MP + 1 DMZ weapons" and exited 0. A well-formed file, a cheerful success line, and completely wrong. It was caught only because the check printed a sample and the sample read `undefined(SNIPER,125)`. It now refuses to write below a floor, and a test asserts that floor rejects exactly that shape.

### Lesson

**A generator that can produce a plausible artifact from a broken query needs a floor, not a success message** — and more generally, every layer added on top of an existing one has to be accompanied by deleting what it replaced, in the same change, or the page grows a stack of things that agree with each other and cost the reader three reads to learn one fact. Both failures share a root: I verified that the new thing worked, and not that the old thing was gone.

Harkirat caught it immediately: "IT IS PAUSED! it's partly built but it's disabled for public viewing." The fix took three independent sources to get precise — `CLAUDE.md`'s own note, `buildLegalPages.js`'s comment ("still built, still deployed and still reachable at /changelog/... what is withdrawn is the landing page's invitation"), and `deploy-site.yml`'s comment — landing on: built and deployed, but nothing links to it, so what's pending is publishing, not building.

**The real lesson wasn't "check code more carefully" — it was "a fact already established in this session can get silently overridden by a later, narrower signal."** Code-exists is not the same question as publicly-reachable, and having correctly used that distinction once doesn't mean it stays applied under later pressure to find something to fix.

Also removed `CHANGELOG.md`'s "Planned & Upcoming" and `CHANGELOG-SUMMARY.md`'s "Coming soon" sections outright, on Harkirat's call after a sequential-thinking deliberation he asked for directly. Both required a three-way manual sync with `docs/ROADMAP.md` that had been silently failing (five shipped features still listed as unbuilt), and the pages have no live audience right now. Verified the removal didn't break anything parsing those files by actually running `buildLegalPages.js` as a probe rather than trusting a docs-audit pass — the chronicle parser explicitly special-cases a `## Coming soon` heading, and confirming that removal was survivable meant running the real consumer, not reading the code and guessing.

Two completeness-sweep passes surfaced the changelog-artifact miss and confirmed three global memory files (`feedback_docs_at_push_time.md`, `project_dior_builds_changelog_system.md`, `project_changelog_redesign.md`, `user_working_agreement.md`) described conventions or statuses that no longer matched — memory files never show up in a git diff, so nothing else would have caught them.

## 2026-08-20 13:17 EDT — Hotpatch v1 built (v3.53.0-pre)

Built Hotpatch v1 from the plan a prior session designed and audited: a way to reload an already-pulled code change into the running bot without a `systemctl restart`. The core idea is one invariant — a reload is sound iff its dependent closure terminates at a late-bound boundary and every member of that closure is either stateless or opts in with a `__hotSwap` contract — and `utils/hotpatch.js` computes that closure from a static require graph read straight off disk, never from `require.cache`, specifically so the answer doesn't depend on what the bot happened to run and can be tested offline.

The one change with real teeth was making `handlers/router.js` resolve its fifteen handler modules at call time instead of capturing them once when the process boots. That's what makes a handler hot-swappable at all, and it's also what keeps the router itself a "boundary" rather than a member of the closure — its own cooldown state never has to be considered when deciding whether a patch is safe. `applyHotpatch` is verify-then-swap and deliberately all-or-nothing: syntax-check every file first, snapshot the old module cache and command collection, swap, validate shape, and roll back to the exact snapshot on any failure. A bot that looks fine while quietly running half-old code is worse than one that's plainly restarting.

Two defects from the earlier falsification pass mattered enough to build the whole design around avoiding them. First, the baseline commit a hotpatch diffs against has to come from `utils/logger.js`'s own require-time commit value, never `git rev-parse HEAD` — Harkirat's actual workflow is pull, then hotpatch, so by the time a hotpatch runs `HEAD` already equals the new commit, and diffing against it would silently report "nothing to patch" over a process still running stale code. Second, a `commands/*.js` swap that changes what Discord shows — a new command, a changed option — has to be refused rather than applied, because the command list only updates through one REST call at boot; swapping the module changes what the code does without changing what Discord displays, and that's a half-shipped feature wearing a green checkmark.

Shipped with zero `__hotSwap` declarations on purpose. Nine files hold nearly all the state that would otherwise block a patch, and the plan's own measurement showed declaring all nine only gets coverage from 46 of 108 modules to 79 — worth doing the first time one of them is actually in the way of a real hot fix, not worth doing speculatively now. Filed both that and the `dior` CLI wrapper as their own queued items.

Also caught, mid-session and unrelated to the feature itself: the self-check hook that nudges a `/rename` string and a model recommendation at the start of every session turned out to only actually check for the model recommendation — so giving that alone silently satisfied both halves of its own stated gate, and the rename string got skipped without anyone noticing until Harkirat did. Flagged as a separate fix rather than folding it into this branch.

## 2026-08-20 14:10 EDT — self-check.sh rename-string gate fixed (v3.53.0-pre)

Found and fixed a real bug in the model-selection gate hook while building Hotpatch v1, on the same branch that had shipped the gate itself hours earlier. The FIRST ACTION message has always asked for two things at the start of a session — a ready-to-paste `/rename` string and a one-line model+effort recommendation — but the "already given, do not repeat it" short-circuit only ever checked for the model recommendation's shape. Stating that alone silently satisfied the whole gate, and the rename string went unrequested for the rest of the session. This wasn't theoretical: it reproduced live, in the same session that was building the hotpatch feature. Fixed by adding a second, deliberately stricter detector for the rename-string's fixed `Model-Effort · Title · Mon DD` shape — stricter than the model-derivation detector on purpose, since a loose separator there false-matched ordinary prose that happened to mention a model name near a date — and requiring both before the gate goes quiet, while keeping the wrong-pick correction independent so it still fires even when the rename string hasn't been given yet.

The investigation also surfaced a second, more interesting failure mode live: quoting the bug's own bad example in a normal explanatory sentence — without knowing to append the hook's existing "MG-EXAMPLE" escape token — made that sentence itself look like a fresh, wrong derivation to the hook's regex, which then "corrected" it again on the very next turn. The fix doesn't need new code for that half; it needs the escape token used correctly, which is now the plan going forward. Also tagged the worked rename-string example inside `docs/SESSION-START.md` with the same escape, since it's read into every session's context and would otherwise have made the new detector permanently fire-blind from turn one — the same trap the model-derivation detector had already been bitten by once.

Filed two findings this session without fixing either, on Harkirat's explicit instruction — the point was to write them down clearly enough that a future session with real time can act on them, not to rush a fix into an already-long session.

The hook had quietly become the real enforcement mechanism instead of a backstop. That fix grows the index, though, which sits in direct tension with an existing deferred item that wants to shrink it for the 25KB budget — filed that tension explicitly rather than picking a side, since it needs a real survey of which lines are thin-and-recurring versus thick-and-fine before either goal should win.

The second finding came from a more pointed exercise: after describing from memory when CHANGELOG, DEVLOG, and the various memory files are supposed to get touched, and what "run the full merge flow" actually means, Harkirat asked for that answer to be stress-tested against what this session had actually done. The stated answer also skipped an entire pipeline stage — CLAUDE.md's own workflow line names a manual dev-bot click-test before the automated suite ever runs, and that got left out entirely, even though this exact PR merged without it (correctly, but only because of an explicit one-time exception, not because that's the default). A few smaller gaps came out of the same pass: a deferred verification step that's written down as history but tracked as open work nowhere, an untested edge case in the version-bump rule that this very branch happens to be the first real test of, and a real question about whether `user_working_agreement.md` has the same soft-trigger staleness risk the MEMORY.md index lines turned out to have.

Wrote the full report to `local/handoff/2026-08-20-record-keeping-timing-audit.md` rather than inline in the deferred list, at Harkirat's request — used the doc-coauthoring skill to draft it and a fresh sub-agent to reader-test it, which caught a real self-contradiction (claiming "findings only" while one small pointer had, in fact, already been added) and a claim that wasn't verifiable from the doc alone. Fixed both before calling it done.

## 2026-08-20 21:56 EDT — The web admin portal, designed — and 28 defects a stranger found in the design (v3.55.0)

Harkirat asked for a web admin portal — everything `/manage` does, plus what a Discord modal physically cannot host — and the session that followed produced four documents, then spent most of its length finding out how wrong they were. The design work is in the spec. What belongs here is the second half.

**Three falsification passes found real defects and shared one blind spot.** They found a fabricated count (twelve `confirm` actions against a real seven), a concurrency model that was wrong because `SeasonalData` is one global document, an inverse that stored a summary instead of the data it destroyed, a headroom figure wrong by 200 MB. Good findings. But every one of them came from re-reading the design. **Not one ran the plan's own code, or grepped a single claim the plan made about code that already exists.**

A context-free reader test — a fresh agent, no session history, told to verify against the source rather than accept — found **28 more**, and every claim spot-checked was confirmed. Four were fatal. `core/ops/index.js` required its entities above `module.exports`, so `registerEntity` was `undefined` inside them: **a TypeError on load, in the plan's first file, which three passes had "reviewed."** The registry conservation test was mathematically incapable of passing — ten mutating draws actions against seven op types over a 1:1 `Map`. Three of seven inverses were destroyed by the validator on the way back in, because `commitSet` validates before applying and the bulk validators re-parsed `payload.text || ''`, so **undo would have silently restored nothing** — and the earlier pass had congratulated itself on storing the full prior array, because its test asserted what `invert()` *returned* and never round-tripped it. And `recordChange` returns `undefined` and takes no session, so `changeIds` was always `[undefined]` and audit rows escaped the transaction they were written inside.

One was a security hole: the Revert control trusted the `bot_` router prefix, which proves *some* `/bot` access rather than page scope — and `handlers/bot.js` says exactly that in a comment at the top of the file, which is why every other mutation there re-checks independently.

Three were plain false claims about other people's code. `patchConsole()` takes zero parameters, so the portal's logging service override was a silent no-op. `scripts/buildLegalPages.js` has no `module.exports`, so the contrast gate an audit entry rested on could not be imported. `handlers/router.js` never imports `ALLOWED_ADMIN_ID`, so a task instructed an edit to a file with nothing to edit. **Each was one `rg` away, and none of the three passes ran it.**

**Then repairing them exposed a bug in this repo's own tooling, and that one is the most useful thing here.** One of the fixes left an orphaned closing fence, giving a plan an odd fence count. `scripts/reflow-prose.mjs` pairs fences positionally, so a later *opening* fence was consumed as an earlier block's close — and every code block after that point was treated as prose. **Nine were collapsed onto single lines, including an entire module implementation.** `verify()` passed, and the reason it passed is the lesson: it compares fenced-block *content*, and under a mispairing it compared the same wrong blocks before and after. **A check that computes its own frame from the corrupted input cannot see the corruption.** It was vacuous in exactly the case it existed for.

The repair was to restore the plan from the last clean commit and re-apply all 22 fixes deterministically rather than un-collapsing nine blocks by hand — surgical repair of scrambled code is where a second, quieter error gets introduced. `reflow-prose.mjs` now reports an odd fence count as its own problem *before* any content comparison, with two test cases including a falsifier for the guard itself, and the guard was proven by disabling it and watching the suite go red.

**Every premise was measured rather than assumed, and one nearly derailed the design for a reason that had nothing to do with it.** Transactions work on M0 — probed on the real cluster inside a throwaway database, 0 of 2 documents after a deliberate rollback and 2 of 2 after a commit. Element-identity concurrency behaves exactly as specified, including a stale prior-value assertion correctly matching nothing. Storage is at 0.15% of 512 MB. The VM has 390 MB available with the bot at 131 MB — not the ~600 MB the spec had derived from figures a month old.

The near-miss: the transaction probe was pinned to `.env.dev` by a guard written to keep it *safe*, and the local database is a **standalone**, where transactions cannot work under any circumstances. It would have failed, and that failure reads exactly like "M0 does not support transactions" — **closing the design's highest-risk question with a confident wrong answer, manufactured by a safety measure.**

**The transferable lesson, stated as a rule rather than a story:** reading a plan is not testing it. Before approving one, run `node -e` on its own snippets and `rg` every claim it makes about existing code. Both are cheap. Neither happened until a stranger did it.

## 2026-08-20 22:15 EDT — Writing a vacuous check minutes after writing about vacuous checks (v3.56.0)

The release immediately before this one carries a long entry about a check that passed while doing nothing — `reflow-prose.mjs`'s `verify()`, which compared fenced-block content and, under a fence mispairing, compared the same *wrong* blocks before and after. Vacuous in exactly the case it existed for.

Then v3.55.0 shipped citing a literal `(#PR)`, and the fix was a new `docs-audit` check. **That check was vacuous twice, in two different ways, within minutes of writing the entry about vacuous checks.**

The first version returned `{ problems: out, examined }`. Every other check in that file returns **`findings`**, and the runner reads only that key — so the check ran, iterated the right files, matched the right pattern, built the right list, and reported nothing. It passed green. The second version returned bare strings; the renderer prints `r.msg`, so findings rendered as `undefined` while still correctly failing the run.

Neither was visible from reading the code. Both took the same thirty seconds to find: inject `(#PR)` into the changelog, run the audit, look at the exit code. **The falsifier is not a formality appended to a finished check — it is the only thing that distinguishes a check from a comment**, and it caught two consecutive failures that careful reading did not.

The underlying defect is worth separating from the fix. **`(#PR)` was structural, not careless.** The workflow composes the changelog entry on the branch as a pre-merge checkpoint, so it can fold into the squash — and at that moment the PR number does not exist. A placeholder is the only honest thing to write. Every audit check passed on it because none of them read whether a citation is a *number*: version coverage saw a version, the hash chain saw a chain, record structure saw a well-formed heading.

So the check is the backstop, and **the actual remedy is ordering**: open the PR first, then write the entry with the real number on the branch. This release did that, which is why its own entry cites `(#165)` and needed no backfill. A rule that requires remembering is worse than a sequence that makes the mistake impossible — and this one cost a whole extra release to learn, which is precisely what the release gate exists to prevent and could not, because the gate reads structure rather than content.

## 2026-08-20 22:55 EDT — A deferred item nobody filed, and two costs that were not costs (v3.57.0)

Harkirat reversed the portal's "ships after v3.0.0" gate the same day it was written. The mechanical part — a dated amendment banner on a frozen spec, decision 4 struck in place with its rationale kept — is the easy half. Two other things came out of it that are worth keeping.

**The reversal surfaced a gap nothing else would have found.** The web admin portal had **no `docs/ROADMAP.md` entry at all.** The spec had deferred it past the launch, so nothing ever filed it — and the single largest piece of designed-but-unbuilt work in the project was invisible to the one file whose entire job is tracking exactly that. Every cross-reference resolved, every audit check passed, and the roadmap was silently incomplete in the way that matters most: **a deferred item that nobody files reads identically to an item that does not exist.** No sweep catches that, because there is nothing to sweep.

**Then I listed three costs of moving the date, and Harkirat corrected two of them.** The systemd unit "moving earlier" was wrong on its face — it is part of building the portal at all, so it happens now or later regardless of when the portal ships. Framing it as a cost of the *timing change* was a category error dressed as diligence: it looks like careful accounting and is actually a made-up number. The `PRIVACY.md` revision was real work but never a blocker, and calling it a cost implied a veto I did not have.

The third was real, and his answer dissolved it: **use the dev bot's OAuth.** `Dioreo (Dev)` already exists for precisely this kind of thing, its secret can be created without touching prod, and Discord accepts an `http://localhost` redirect URI — so no tunnel, no TLS, and the portal is buildable end to end before prod's credential exists at all. What I had written as a hard dependency on him was a dependency on *one* application, and there were two.

**The transferable shape:** when you enumerate the costs of a decision, check each one against the counterfactual — *would this still happen if we did the other thing?* Two of my three failed that test, and a cost that survives both branches is not a cost of the choice. It is just work, and listing it makes a decision look more expensive than it is, which is its own way of arguing without saying so.

**One more thing this release caught, about itself.** The first draft of its DEVLOG title was stamped `23:00 EDT` while the clock read `22:54` — six minutes ahead, estimated rather than read. `timestamp-check.sh` denied the write outright, which is the behaviour that hook was built for after 2026-08-02 put thirty fabricated stamps into docs, memory, a released CHANGELOG and a git tag. But the same stamp had *already* reached the spec, the ROADMAP and the CHANGELOG in a commit that went out before the DEVLOG write triggered the gate. **A hook that fires on one file does not protect the other five in the same batch** — it caught the instance, not the habit, and the habit is the thing that produces a plausible number instead of reading the clock sitting one tool call away.

## 2026-08-21 12:31 EDT — Parallelizing the test suite, and the bugs sharding almost shipped (v3.58.0)

Ported speed wins from the gif-background-remover skill's own harness work — `analysis_cache.py`'s session-scoped content-hash caching and `run_populations.py`'s parallel scoring — into this repo's dev-loop scripts. Measured first, then built: `npm run check` (`node --check` across every JS file) was 44.6s fully serial; `xargs -P 8` (the same mechanism the source repo used, and already validated on this machine's 6-performance/2-efficiency-core split before adopting it) got it to 1.7–5.0s. `.claude/hooks/run-all-tests.sh`'s 28 hook self-tests were 53.4s serial, badly skewed (3 of 28 tests — each an `rg` sweep over the real repo tree, repeated per test case — were 64% of the total). A first draft hand-rolled a bash busy-poll scheduler to work around bash 3.2 lacking `wait -n`; code review correctly flagged that `xargs -P` (already proven on the `check` script) solved the identical problem with no poll-loop CPU waste, so the busy-poll version was replaced before it shipped.

The bigger piece was `scripts/docs-audit.test.mjs` — the self-test that proves every one of `docs-audit.mjs`'s checks (the repo's documentation-invariant auditor, and the only script here wired into CI as a hard merge gate) can actually FAIL, not just pass silently. Its cold-run cost was ~110–115s. Instrumented timing (a scratch copy with timing wrappers around every `proves()`/`provesSilent()`/`provesBaselineClean()` call) found something different from the hooks case: a completely FLAT distribution — 68 of 71 checks at 1.5–5.2s each, median 3.1s, no long pole at all. That shape parallelizes close to linearly, so this got process-sharded across N node subprocesses instead of the hooks fix's `xargs -P` shell-level approach, because unlike a directory of independent hook scripts, these 71 checks live as sequential statements inside ONE file sharing module-level `passed`/`failures` counters — safe parallelization meant splitting them across separate OS processes (each with its own counters, aggregated after), never threading async concurrency through 850 lines of an audit-of-record file.

**The key insight that made sharding safe**: `proven.add(checkId)` — the line that registers a check as "has a test" for the file's own coverage meta-check — stays the unconditional first statement of `proves()`/`provesSilent()`, always executed before any shard-skip decision. That means every shard's own `proven` Set ends up complete regardless of which subset it actually runs, so the coverage check is correct in every shard with zero cross-process merging needed for it specifically. A structural guarantee, not something a shard-count change could silently break — except that "structural guarantee" turned out to be the exact phrase that needed the most scrutiny.

**Two full rounds of 8-angle parallel code review, and a third bug found by neither of them.** Round 1 (on `testCache.mjs`, the new session-scoped cache, and the hooks parallelization): `HOOK_TEST_JOBS=0` deadlocked the suite forever (verified by direct reproduction — a bash `while [ jobs -ge JOBS ]` gate with JOBS=0 never opens); the cache's own self-test leaked real cache files into the actual repo because `testCache.mjs` derives its cache directory from the SCRIPT's own file location via `import.meta.url`, not from `cwd` — passing a different `cwd` to the test's subprocess call did nothing to isolate it, and the header comment claiming isolation was simply wrong; a cache entry was keyed on input-file hash alone, never the wrapped command, so editing `package.json`'s command string without touching the hashed files would replay a stale pass for code that was never executed; a `spawnSync` failure (missing binary, bad permissions) was silently downgraded to a bare exit code with zero diagnostic text.

Round 2 (on the docs-audit sharding specifically) found the session's most severe defect: a malformed `DOCS_AUDIT_TEST_SHARD` value — missing the slash, or a stray integer someone confused with the sibling `DOCS_AUDIT_TEST_JOBS` knob — made `SHARD_N` destructure to `NaN`, which made every `__mine()` call return `false` for every single check. Every expensive body got skipped. But `proven.add(checkId)` still ran unconditionally, so the coverage meta-check still reported full coverage, and the file printed `✅ all 0 checks proven capable of failing.` and exited 0 — a silent, self-congratulating false pass in the one file whose entire purpose is catching exactly that failure shape. Fixed two ways: parse-time validation that refuses to run at all on a malformed shard spec, and a cross-shard invariant added to the aggregator — every shard now reports its own total check-slot count (`__idx`'s final value) alongside its pass/fail counts, and the aggregator asserts every shard agrees on that total AND that executed checks summed across all shards equal it. Neither existed in the version code review actually reviewed; both were added specifically because "the shards structurally cannot disagree" turned out to be reasoning, not proof.

The third bug was in the fix for the second one, and neither review round caught it — it surfaced only when verifying the fix against its own worst case. The new invariant's counter increment lived inside `SHARD_N <= 1 || __idx++ % SHARD_N === SHARD_I` — a `||` short-circuit, so when `SHARD_N <= 1` (the default, unsharded path — the ONE path every existing convention assumes is safe by definition) the right-hand side, including `__idx++`, never evaluates at all. The unsharded run's own diagnostic line printed `SHARD_RESULT 0 1 71 0 0` — 71 checks correctly passed, but a total-slot-count of 0, which is exactly the mismatch the new invariant exists to catch. Had this shipped, the very first time anyone ran `DOCS_AUDIT_TEST_JOBS=1` — a single real shard, `SHARD_N=1` — the wrapper would have seen "71 executed, 0 expected" and reported a false failure, in the one file that exists to make failure reports trustworthy. Caught by running that exact configuration and reading the printed number, not by re-reading the logic and reasoning it through — the same lesson the file's OWN header repeats about itself: a guard that has never been watched to fail is not a guard, and "it obviously can't disagree" is not the same claim as "I ran it and it didn't."

Net result: `npm run check` 44.6s → 1.7–5.0s; `test:hooks` 53.4s cold → ~16–21s, 0.3s warm; `docs:audit:test` ~110–115s cold → ~40–55s, 0.25–0.5s warm; full `npm test` ~92s cold → ~28–31s warm. All measured with a concurrent unrelated session sharing the same machine's CPU throughout, so the absolute numbers understate the clean-machine case — the relative wins held consistently across every re-run regardless.

## 2026-08-21 19:05 EDT — The operation core's own sharp edges — plan 2 wired, and what a second caller exposed (v3.59.0)

Plan 2 of the portal operation-core refactor finished this session: `calendar`/`loadouts`/`patchnotes`/`season`/`announcements` all now route through `core/changeset.js`'s `commitSet`, the same transactional op-plus-inverse pattern plan 1 built for `draws`. The old in-memory Undo store is gone — revert lives permanently on `/bot analytics`' Changes page now, backed by Mongo instead of a Map that forgot everything on restart.

The real story of this session is what the review found once all six entities were sharing one mechanism. Two bugs lived in the operation core itself, not in any single entity, and both were the kind that only show up once a SECOND caller exercises a code path the first caller never touched:

`commitSet` validated its `ops` array before flattening it. That was fine for every direct call — a handler always passes a flat array of plain op objects. But `core/revert.js`'s `revertChange` calls `commitSet([row.inverse], ...)`, and several ops' `invert()` legitimately return an ARRAY (a purge needs to restore two categories at once; a bulk-delete needs one op per category). So `ops` became `[[opA, opB]]` — one element that is itself an array — and `resolveOp(op.type)` on an array element sees `op.type === undefined` and throws. Every revert of a bulk-delete, a purge, or (the moment plan 2's loadout bulk-invert landed) a bulk loadout import failed unconditionally, with a generic "unknown op type" error that gave no hint the actual cause was an ordering bug in the validation step itself.

The second one was worse in a quieter way: `pageForOp` derives a `ChangeLog` row's `page` field from the op's registered `action`, which can be an array when one op type serves two pages — loadouts registers everything under `['loadouts_mp:x', 'loadouts_dmz:x']`, MP always listed first. `pageForOp` just took index `[0]`. Every DMZ loadout mutation — add, edit, delete, every bulk operation — got stamped `page: 'loadouts_mp'` in its audit row. That's not just a cosmetic mislabel: `core/revert.js`'s access check reads that exact field to decide whether the clicking admin holds a grant for the page a change belongs to. An admin scoped only to `loadouts_dmz` would have been wrongly blocked from reverting their own DMZ changes, while an admin scoped only to `loadouts_mp` could have reverted a DMZ change they hold no grant for at all — a real privilege boundary riding silently on a `[0]` that should have asked which mode the op actually targeted.

Neither bug would have been caught by the existing test suite, because the tests exercise each op's `validate`/`apply`/`invert` in isolation — nothing round-trips an inverse back through `commitSet` itself, and nothing asserts `pageForOp`'s output against a DMZ-flavored op. The branch-wide review (13 finder agents, split across both plan 1's and plan 2's diff ranges, two of which hit a session limit mid-run and had to be re-launched once the limit reset) is what surfaced both — not by running the code, but by tracing `revertChange`'s call shape against every `invert()` in the codebase and asking "what does this function assume about its input that isn't guaranteed."

Eight more real bugs came out of the same pass, most of them the unglamorous kind that only bite on an unlucky ordering: `draw.bulkAdd` computed its new elements' ids by tail-slicing the array AFTER re-sorting it by date — correct only if every newly-added draw sorts after every existing one, and silently wrong (grabbing some unrelated pre-existing draw's id instead) the moment it doesn't, which then feeds straight into that bulk-add's own revert target. `loadout.bulkAdd`/`bulkReplace`'s invert only knew how to delete the docs it had CREATED, leaving anything the same paste OVERWROTE in place permanently stuck at its new values with no error telling anyone the revert was incomplete. A transaction-retry bug where `changeIds`/`results` accumulator arrays lived outside the retried `withTransaction` callback, so a MongoDB transient-transaction retry (plausible here, since `SeasonalData` is one shared document under write contention) could double-count entries from an aborted first attempt. And a genuinely embarrassing one: the `docs:audit` npm script itself had been silently dropped by an early merge-conflict resolution at this session's own start, breaking CI's audit gate for 9+ commits before the review's conventions-angle agent caught it.

A second, smaller pass fixed 7 lower-severity findings from the same review rather than leaving them as a "known cleanup" note nobody would act on — a duplicated `resortByDate` hoisted to a shared home, a fragile post-commit re-read replaced by surfacing the data on the op's own result, a 31-times-repeated error-extraction expression collapsed into one helper, some dead exports removed, one doc section that still described a retired mechanism corrected. None of them changed behavior; all of them make the next person's read of this code shorter.

The throughline, if there is one: an operation-core pattern is only as safe as its SECOND real caller. Plan 1 built and tested `commitSet` against one entity calling it one way. Plan 2 added five more callers with genuinely different shapes — multi-page action registration, array-valued inverses, bulk upserts that both create and update — and every one of those differences is exactly where the two core-level bugs were hiding. The lesson isn't "test more" in the abstract; it's that a shared mechanism's test suite needs at least one caller from every *shape* of caller it will actually have, not just coverage of the mechanism's own internal logic in isolation.

## 2026-08-22 23:40 EDT — Five deferred items, two false premises checked, and one spec reframed mid-draft (v3.64.0-pre)

Five deferred items in one sitting, and the two framed as "confirmed X, not Y" both survived the check — which is the only reason the rest of the session was spent on the right problems.

The loadout bulk format was filed as a taste complaint: *"why are some things in 1 line, while other things are in their own lines... there's no intuitiveness to its structure at all."* Reading it to answer that question turned up a real defect underneath. `parseBulkLoadoutList` REQUIRED Mode as pipe-segment 3 — a block omitting it was rejected outright — while `core/ops/loadouts.js` overwrote it with the page's mode unconditionally. Verified live before touching anything: a block typed `DMZ` pasted into the MP page parsed with zero errors and saved as MP, and the exporter emitted that same Mode, so exporting DMZ builds and pasting them on the MP page silently reassigned every one of them. The parser demanded a field whose value it discarded; the exporter round-tripped a value that could not survive the trip. The honest answer to "why is there no intuitiveness" was that there was no principle to state. There is one now — identity on line one, every optional field on its own labelled line, everything else an attachment — and the old format is rejected loudly rather than half-parsed.

The retry pattern is the cross-cutting one: a failed `/manage` submit now replies with what you typed, reconstructed in that entity's own bulk format, plus a button that reopens the same modal already refilled. The snippet is in the bulk format rather than a raw field dump specifically so it pastes back into *something*, and each format is pinned by a test that re-imports it through the real parser — asserting the snippet's shape would have passed against a format the parser rejects. The button exists because Discord cannot answer a MODAL_SUBMIT with another modal; that is a platform toll `pendingManageEdits` already pays elsewhere.

Loadout images by URL needed no new modal field at all. The Cloudinary Image Key input tells a Public ID from a pasted URL by its scheme, which mattered because the modal was already at Discord's hard 5-field cap. On Edit the upload targets the build's existing public_id, so replacing a picture never breaks a reference.

The `/bot analytics` overhaul got a spec and an 8-task plan rather than code, and then got reframed mid-drafting. The first draft assumed Discord had to remain a full analytics dashboard and spent its effort making five dense pages distinguishable. Harkirat's correction — the portal now carries the depth, so make Discord a glance — was the better answer to the original complaint, and the premise the first draft never stated out loud was exactly the thing a falsification pass is supposed to catch. It took him noticing rather than the pass. Before writing "move it to the portal" into a spec, `portal/ui/analytics.js` was confirmed to actually exist and to carry revert; otherwise rule 0 would have deleted real capability and called it design.

DMZ Loadouts was asked about and deferred by name, so it stays open on purpose with the menu of real gameplay differences recorded against it for whenever it reopens.

The investigation mattered because it ruled out the cheap fix.
